<span class="ct-node-title-full">What Captured Weapons Really Reveal</span>
<span class="ct-node-summary">Reverse engineering foreign military technology is the organised study of captured, bought, recovered or otherwise obtained weapons and systems to understand how an adversary’s equipment works, how it can be countered, and what lessons can be absorbed into one’s own defence industry. It is not simply copying.</span>
</span>

</div>
<span class="ct-node-content">
<span class="ct-node-label">Copy vs Redesign</span>
<span class="ct-node-title-full">Was the Tu 4 really an exact copy?</span>
<span class="ct-node-summary">The Tu-4 looked like a B-29, but Soviet engineers had to translate the bomber into a different industrial system.</span>
</span>
</a>
<div class="home-vertical-card-actions">
<a class="topic-card-link home-vertical-read-more" href="{{ 'copy-vs-redesign/' | relative_url }}" title="Was the Tu 4 really an exact copy?" aria-label="Read more about Was the Tu 4 really an exact copy?">Read more</a>
</div>

</div>
<span class="ct-node-content">
<span class="ct-node-label">Soviet Substitutes</span>
<span class="ct-node-title-full">What the Soviets had to replace</span>
<span class="ct-node-summary">The Tu-4 had to use Soviet powerplants, guns and equipment, proving that copying often means replacing what cannot be reproduced.</span>
</span>
</a>

<span class="ct-node-title-full">Why measurements made copying harder</span>

</span>
